\section*{Measuring Digital Self-Efficacy in International Large-Scale
Assessments: A bi-dimensional comparative scale Between ICILS and
PISA}\label{measuring-digital-self-efficacy-in-international-large-scale-assessments-a-bi-dimensional-comparative-scale-between-icils-and-pisa}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Measuring Digital Self-Efficacy in
International Large-Scale Assessments: A bi-dimensional comparative
scale Between ICILS and PISA}

Daniel Miranda\footnote{Assistant professor, Psychology Department,
  University of Chile.
  \href{mailto:danmiranda@uchile.cl}{damiran1@uc.cl}.}, Ismael
Aguayo\footnote{Bsc (c) Sociology, Sociology Department, University of
  Chile.
  \href{mailto:ismael.aguayo@ug.uchile.cl}{\nolinkurl{ismael.aguayo@ug.uchile.cl}}},
Nicolás Tobar\footnote{Msc (c) Political Science, Faculty of Government,
  University of Chile.
  \href{mailto:nicolas.tobar@ug.uchile.cl}{nicolas.tobar@ug.uchile.cl.}},
Tomás Urzúa\footnote{Msc (c) Social Sciences, Sociology Department,
  University of Chile.
  \href{mailto:tomas.urzua@ug.uchile.cl}{\nolinkurl{tomas.urzua@ug.uchile.cl}}}
and Juan Carlos Castillo\footnote{Full professor, Sociology Department,
  University of Chile.
  \href{mailto:juancastillov@uchile.cl}{\nolinkurl{juancastillov@uchile.cl}}
  - \url{jc-castillo.com}}

\textbf{Abstract}

Understanding students' beliefs about their capabilities with digital
technologies, commonly referred to as digital self-efficacy (DSE), is
critical for interpreting the levels of digital competence across
educational systems. Depending on how studies conceptualize and measure
the construct, they can significantly affect their results. The two most
relevant International Large-Scale Assessments (ILSAs) that address this
topic differ in how DSE is measured. The International Computer and
Information Literacy Study (ICILS) adopts a bidimensional approach,
while the Programme for International Student Assessment (PISA) treats
DSE as an unidimensional construct, despite relying on highly similar
digital tasks. This lack of a common definitions leads to different
interpretations of the construct's distribution across key determinants
of inequality, such as gender or country. Moreover, some studies
operationalize the PISA self-efficacy construct as two-dimensional
without first examining its psychometric properties. This study examines
whether a bidimensional structure of DSE can be empirically supported in
both ILSAs. We employ multi-group confirmatory factor analysis to assess
the validity and comparability of specialized and general dimensions of
DSE, and test measurement invariance across countries and genders. The
results support a two-factor structure, full gender invariance in both
datasets, full country invariance in PISA and scalar country invariance
in ICILS. A descriptive exploration shows, consistently across countries
and studies, smaller gender differences in general DSE, while
specialized DSE systematically favors boys. These findings refine the
interpretation of DSE in ILSAs, propose a comparable scale for future
research, and emphasize the importance of supporting DSE dimensionality
with empirical evidence.

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction}

Digital competence has become essential for learning and participation
in contemporary societies. However, research increasingly shows that
mastering digital technologies requires more than access to devices or
procedural knowledge; it also involves attitudinal dispositions such as
confidence, persistence, and adaptability in complex digital
environments (\citeproc{ref-dijk_digital_2020}{Dijk, 2020};
\citeproc{ref-europeancommissionjointresearchcentre_digcomp_2022}{European
Commission Joint Research Centre, 2022};
\citeproc{ref-lythreatis_digital_2022}{Lythreatis et al., 2022}).
Digital self-efficacy (DSE), defined as the belief in one's ability to
accomplish tasks and overcome obstacles in digital contexts, has
therefore emerged as a central construct for understanding the
development of digital competences
(\citeproc{ref-bandura_selfefficacy_1995}{Bandura, 1995};
\citeproc{ref-ulfert-blank_assessing_2022}{Ulfert-Blank \& Schmidt,
2022}). Compared with purely skill-based measures, DSE captures
perceived capability in ways that help explain why some learners
navigate digital transformations successfully while others struggle
(\citeproc{ref-rohatgi_role_2016}{Rohatgi et al., 2016};
\citeproc{ref-scherer_revisiting_2015}{Scherer \& Siddiq, 2015}).

A growing body of cross-national research has examined how levels of DSE
and related inequalities vary across countries, typically attributing
these differences to broader sociocultural and educational contexts
(\citeproc{ref-campos_digital_2024}{Campos \& Scherer, 2024};
\citeproc{ref-hatlevik_students_2018}{Hatlevik et al., 2018}). Yet,
despite the prominence of descriptive cross-national comparisons,
relatively little attention has been devoted to the measurement
comparability of DSE across populations. This gap may limit the
interpretability and robustness of claims about cross-national
differences. Without explicit tests of comparability, it is difficult to
determine whether observed differences reflect substantive disparities
or methodological artefacts
(\citeproc{ref-leitgob_measurement_2023}{Leitgöb et al., 2023}).

International large-scale assessments (ILSAs) offer a unique opportunity
to address issues of measurement comparability, although existing
instruments are not fully aligned. For example, the International
Computer and Information Literacy Study (ICILS) distinguishes between
general and specialized DSE using a bidimensional framework
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_international_2025}{Fraillon, 2025}), whereas
the Programme for International Student Assessment (PISA) has
traditionally adopted a unidimensional approach by aggregating digital
self-confidence into a single scale (\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2023}{OECD,
2023b}). These differences are consequential: when closely related
constructs are operationalized differently across assessments,
cross-study comparisons become difficult, and the cumulative evidence on
the social determinants of digital competence remains fragmented.

Gender differences in DSE represent one of the most salient areas of
focus across educational systems. Historically, women have reported
lower levels of confidence and motivation in digital environments,
particularly in domains associated with STEM or advanced technical
applications (\citeproc{ref-cai_gender_2017}{Cai et al., 2017};
\citeproc{ref-hargittai_differences_2006}{Hargittai \& Shafer, 2006}).
More recent research suggests that, although women often match or
surpass men in basic digital tasks related to information navigation,
communication, and content creation, they tend to report lower
self-efficacy in more specialized areas such as programming or data
analysis (\citeproc{ref-gebhardt_gender_2019}{Gebhardt et al., 2019}).
This pattern supports a bidimensional conceptualization of DSE, in which
gender inequalities manifest differently across domains. Importantly,
the magnitude of these gender differences varies across countries
(\citeproc{ref-campos_digital_2023}{Campos \& Scherer, 2023}),
indicating that both contextual factors and measurement choices play a
role.

There are empirical reasons to speculate that the PISA battery
implicitly captures the two dimensions of DSE proposed by ICILS, even
though these dimensions are not explicitly theorized in the PISA
framework. These reasons include high similarity in item wording,
comparable covariance structures among items, and similar associations
with key external variables. Building on this assumption, recent studies
have applied a bidimensional DSE framework to PISA data, despite the
fact that this extrapolation has not been tested in detail or
theoretically discussed (e.g.,
\citeproc{ref-hristov_understanding_2026}{Hristov et al., 2026}). Such
studies rely on international data that require a high degree of
stability in the interpretation of scale meaning across diverse contexts
to ensure comparability. This highlights the need for research that
explicitly addresses this issue.

In this study, we argue that a two-dimensional structure of
DSE---general and specialized---can be identified and meaningfully
compared across ICILS 2023 and PISA 2022. Specifically, we (i) test the
fit of a bidimensional DSE model in both assessments, (ii) evaluate
measurement invariance across gender and countries, and (iii) examine
cross-national variation in gender differences in DSE in each study.
Finally, we propose a comparable scale to explore parallelisms in DSE
across the two assessments.

\section{Self-efficacy and Digital
Technologies}\label{self-efficacy-and-digital-technologies}

\subsubsection{Self-efficacy and digital
learning}\label{self-efficacy-and-digital-learning}

Self-efficacy, originally formulated by Bandura
(\citeproc{ref-bandura_selfefficacy_1982}{1982}), refers to individuals'
judgments about their capability to execute the actions required to
manage prospective situations (p.~122). This construct is central to
human agency, as it helps explain how people approach challenges,
sustain effort in the face of setbacks, and build competence in complex
domains. Self-efficacy beliefs support goal setting, persistence, and
the interpretation of performance feedback, thereby shaping learning and
achievement across a wide range of contexts
(\citeproc{ref-bandura_selfefficacy_1995}{Bandura, 1995};
\citeproc{ref-bandura_selfefficacy_1997}{Bandura, 1997};
\citeproc{ref-steele_stereotype_1995}{Steele \& Aronson, 1995}).

Self-efficacy is particularly relevant for understanding learning
processes involving digital technologies
(\citeproc{ref-aesaert_accuracy_2017}{Aesaert et al., 2017};
\citeproc{ref-hatlevik_digital_2015}{Hatlevik et al., 2015};
\citeproc{ref-moos_learning_2009}{Moos \& Azevedo, 2009}). A substantial
body of research has examined how self-efficacy beliefs relate to the
adoption of new technologies and the development of digital competence;
however, measurement approaches remain fragmented. Early work focused on
general computer self-efficacy and confidence in the use of specific
software applications (\citeproc{ref-compeau_computer_1995}{Compeau \&
Higgins, 1995}; \citeproc{ref-durndell_computer_2002}{Durndell \& Haag,
2002}; \citeproc{ref-moos_learning_2009}{Moos \& Azevedo, 2009};
\citeproc{ref-weigel_technical_2014}{Weigel \& Hazen, 2014}), whereas
other strands emphasized self-efficacy for internet navigation and
online communication (\citeproc{ref-eastin_internet_2000a}{Eastin \&
LaRose, 2000}; \citeproc{ref-hsu_internet_2004}{Hsu \& Chiu, 2004};
\citeproc{ref-liang_internet_2008}{Liang \& Tsai, 2008}). This diversity
of operationalizations highlights an ongoing challenge: although
self-efficacy in digital environments is theoretically meaningful, it is
empirically sensitive to how digital tasks are defined and grouped.

\subsubsection{From ICT self-efficacy to Digital Self-Efficacy
(DSE)}\label{from-ict-self-efficacy-to-digital-self-efficacy-dse}

As digital technologies became increasingly hybrid, combining
computational components with networked social interaction, research
converged around broader constructs such as ICT self-efficacy, which
integrate internet-based tasks with more technical and computer-related
activities within a single line of inquiry
(\citeproc{ref-aesaert_exploring_2014}{Aesaert \& van Braak, 2014};
\citeproc{ref-papastergiou_enhancing_2010}{Papastergiou, 2010}).
Considering this, several studies adopted a bidimensional approach that
distinguishes between (a) general digital tasks related to information
processing, communication, and content creation, and (b) more advanced
or specialized computational tasks such as programming or web
development (\citeproc{ref-aesaert_exploring_2014}{Aesaert \& van Braak,
2014}; \citeproc{ref-hatlevik_students_2018}{Hatlevik et al., 2018};
\citeproc{ref-rohatgi_role_2016}{Rohatgi et al., 2016}). Studies
adopting this framework have consistently identified contrasting
associations with key variables related to competence development:
self-efficacy in general digital tasks tends to align positively with
broader digital proficiency, whereas self-efficacy in specialized
domains often shows weaker, null, or even inverse relationships
(\citeproc{ref-aydin_multilevel_2022}{Aydin, 2022};
\citeproc{ref-gebhardt_gender_2019}{Gebhardt et al., 2019};
\citeproc{ref-rohatgi_role_2016}{Rohatgi et al., 2016}).

More recently, Ulfert-Blank \& Schmidt
(\citeproc{ref-ulfert-blank_assessing_2022}{2022}) argued that
traditional ICT self-efficacy instruments may struggle to keep pace with
rapid technological change. Because many measures are tied to specific
applications, interfaces, or devices, they can become outdated as
digital systems evolve. In response, Ulfert-Blank \& Schmidt
(\citeproc{ref-ulfert-blank_assessing_2022}{2022}) proposed the
construct of digital self-efficacy (DSE), defined as individuals'
confidence in their ability to organize and execute the actions required
to successfully perform digital tasks across diverse and changing
contexts. This conceptualization draws on the European Digital
Competence Framework (DigComp)
(\citeproc{ref-europeancommissionjointresearchcentre_digcomp_2022}{European
Commission Joint Research Centre, 2022}), emphasizing that self-efficacy
measures in digital environments should remain coherent across a broad
range of tasks and technological ecologies. Accordingly, we adopt the
label digital self-efficacy rather than ICT self-efficacy to highlight
that the target of the construct is not a specific set of devices or
applications.

\subsection{Operationalizations of DSE in international large-scale
assessments}\label{operationalizations-of-dse-in-international-large-scale-assessments}

A major motivation for this study is that large-scale assessments
operationalize digital self-efficacy in ways that differ substantially
in both content and dimensionality. Such differences can influence how
cross-national inequalities are interpreted and whether findings
accumulate meaningfully across studies. Two prominent international
large-scale assessments are ICILS and PISA, both of which include
student-reported measures related to digital self-efficacy.

\subsubsection{ICILS and the bidimensional approach to
DSE}\label{icils-and-the-bidimensional-approach-to-dse}

The International Computer and Information Literacy Study (ICILS),
conducted by the International Association for the Evaluation of
Educational Achievement (IEA), is the primary large-scale assessment
that adopts and maintains a bidimensional approach to ICT self-efficacy.
ICILS assesses students' Computer and Information Literacy (CIL) through
standardized performance tests while also collecting survey data on
digital-related attitudes and background variables. The study has been
administered three times (2013, 2018, and 2023), using nationally
representative samples of eighth-grade students across multiple
educational systems.

ICILS draws on Bandura's conceptualization of self-efficacy as perceived
capability to execute courses of action
(\citeproc{ref-bandura_perceived_1993}{Bandura, 1993}) and applies it to
students' confidence in performing ICT-related tasks
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_iea_2025}{Fraillon et al., 2025}). Since its
inception, ICILS has distinguished between (a) self-efficacy in general
ICT tasks, defined as confidence in everyday digital activities such as
information retrieval, online communication, and content creation, and
(b) self-efficacy in specialized ICT tasks, defined as confidence in
more technically advanced activities such as programming or database
management (\citeproc{ref-fraillon_iea_2025}{Fraillon et al., 2025}).
This distinction reflects the idea that confidence in routine digital
engagement may function differently from confidence in high-level
technical creation.

Although the bidimensional structure has remained stable, the
composition and wording of item batteries have evolved across cycles to
mirror the changing digital landscape. Between 2013 and 2018, the
terminology shifted from ``basic'' and ``advanced'' to ``general'' and
``specialized'', and items were recalibrated accordingly. The 2023 cycle
introduced more substantial modifications: the specialized dimension was
narrowed to focus more strongly on visual coding, text-based
programming, and web creation, moving away from earlier items related to
local networks or database management, while the general dimension
incorporated updated items reflecting contemporary information literacy
demands (e.g., identifying original sources). Item stems were also
revised to reduce explicit references to ``computers'', acknowledging
the diversity of devices used by students.

Psychometric validation of the bidimensional ICILS self-efficacy
framework has been consistently documented across cycles, although the
methodological strategies have evolved. In the 2013 and 2018 cycles,
dimensionality was examined using exploratory procedures in field trials
and later confirmed through Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) within a
Structural Equation Modeling (SEM) framework
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_icils_2015}{Fraillon et al., 2015}). The 2018
cycle extended this approach by using multi-group CFA to evaluate
measurement invariance across educational systems
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_iea_2020}{Fraillon et al., 2020a}). In the 2023
cycle, validation shifted toward an Item Response Theory (IRT)
framework, using Partial Credit Models (PCM) and multi-group PCM to
assess fit and cross-national comparability
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_iea_2025}{Fraillon et al., 2025}). Taken
together, evidence from ICILS supports the operationalization of digital
self-efficacy as two related but distinct dimensions.

The empirical distinctiveness of the bidimensional framework is
reinforced by the diverging relationships that general and specialized
self-efficacy exhibit with digital competence and gender across diverse
educational systems. Consistent analyses from ICILS international
reports and independent studies demonstrate that self-efficacy in
general ICT tasks functions as a robust, positive predictor of CIL
achievement (\citeproc{ref-aydin_multilevel_2022}{Aydin, 2022};
\citeproc{ref-fraillon_students_2020}{Fraillon et al., 2020b};
\citeproc{ref-rohatgi_role_2016}{Rohatgi et al., 2016};
\citeproc{ref-rozman_students_2025}{Rožman et al., 2025}). Conversely,
self-efficacy in specialized tasks has repeatedly shown a null or even
negative association with CIL performance. This discrepancy is often
attributed to a ``misalignment'' between the high-level technical skills
encompassed by specialized ICT self-efficacy (e.g., programming) and the
information-management focus of CIL assessments, in which technical
expertise does not necessarily translate into informational competence
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_iea_2020}{Fraillon et al., 2020a};
\citeproc{ref-rohatgi_role_2016}{Rohatgi et al., 2016}). Furthermore,
this distinction reveals important gender dynamics: while boys
consistently report higher specialized self-efficacy, this gap
disappears in the general dimension
(\citeproc{ref-gebhardt_gender_2019}{Gebhardt et al., 2019}), even
though girls outperform boys in CIL scores across almost all educational
systems (\citeproc{ref-kennedy_relationships_2025}{Kennedy et al.,
2025}).

\subsubsection{PISA and the unidimensional approach to
DSE}\label{pisa-and-the-unidimensional-approach-to-dse}

The Programme for International Student Assessment (PISA), coordinated
by the OECD, evaluates 15-year-old students' ability to apply knowledge
and skills in reading, mathematics, and science to real-life challenges
(\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2023b}{OECD, 2023a}). Since its first cycle in
2000, PISA has expanded its contextual questionnaires and frameworks to
capture students' exposure to and engagement with digital technologies.
The latest published cycle (PISA 2022) includes measures related to ICT
use, access, and digital competence (\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2023}{OECD,
2023b}).

In PISA, self-efficacy related to digital tasks functions as an
important indicator of students' perceived capacity to perform digital
activities, particularly given the absence of an ICT performance
assessment comparable to ICILS. PISA's framework aligns with DigComp by
covering multiple competence areas, including information,
communication, content creation, safety, and problem solving
(\citeproc{ref-europeancommissionjointresearchcentre_digcomp_2022}{European
Commission Joint Research Centre, 2022};
\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2023}{OECD, 2023b}). Consequently, the PISA
digital self-efficacy battery includes items that extend beyond the
ICILS operationalization, incorporating tasks such as protecting data
privacy or representing computational solutions as algorithms.

Despite overlap in item scope and wording, as can be observed in
Table~\ref{tbl-comparison}, PISA differs from ICILS in its treatment of
dimensionality. PISA operationalizes digital self-efficacy as a
unidimensional construct, treating variation across heterogeneous tasks
as reflecting a single latent continuum rather than distinct underlying
dimensions (\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2018_framework}{OECD, 2019},
\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2023}{2023b}). PISA's psychometric validation
relies primarily on IRT scaling, often using Partial Credit Models,
supported by exploratory analyses such as principal component analyses
to justify the unidimensional structure
(\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2024}{OECD, 2024}). Scale quality is evaluated
using item-fit indices, category functioning, reliability indicators,
and multi-group IRT procedures to support cross-national comparability
(\citeproc{ref-adams_wu_wilson_2012_conquest}{Adams et al., 2012};
\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2024}{OECD, 2024}).

However, unlike ICILS, multidimensional alternatives for digital
self-efficacy have not been systematically tested within the PISA
framework, nor has it been explicitly examined whether item groupings
comparable to ICILS' general and specialized domains form empirically
distinguishable latent factors. As a result, although PISA's digital
self-efficacy scale may demonstrate strong psychometric properties under
a unidimensional specification, the question of whether students'
digital self-efficacy is more accurately represented by a bidimensional
structure remains open and requires explicit empirical assessment.

\subsubsection{Implications for cross-national and gender
research}\label{implications-for-cross-national-and-gender-research}

The unidimensional operationalization of digital self-efficacy has
substantive consequences for how digital inequality is described and
interpreted. Studies using the PISA ICT self-efficacy battery as a
single latent construct consistently report significant associations
with gender, socioeconomic status, ICT use, motivational orientations
toward technology, and academic achievement in reading, mathematics, and
science (\citeproc{ref-chen_ictrelated_2020}{Chen \& Hu, 2020};
\citeproc{ref-hori_impact_2021}{Hori \& Fujii, 2021};
\citeproc{ref-kunina-habenicht_ict_2020}{Kunina-Habenicht \& Goldhammer,
2020}; \citeproc{ref-yu_ict_2022}{Yu \& Hu, 2022}). These findings
suggest that digital self-efficacy, as measured in PISA, functions as a
meaningful predictor of educational outcomes
(\citeproc{ref-yu_ict_2022}{Yu \& Hu, 2022}). At the same time,
collapsing heterogeneous digital tasks into a single continuum implies
functional equivalence between qualitatively different forms of digital
engagement, ranging from routine information and communication tasks to
more specialized and computationally demanding activities
(\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2023}{OECD, 2023b}).

Emerging evidence suggests that the heterogeneity of digital tasks may
matter. While self-efficacy related to general or information-oriented
digital skills tends to show robust positive associations with
achievement and background variables, self-efficacy related to more
specialized or computational domains often displays weaker, null, or
even reverse relationships
(\citeproc{ref-hristov_understanding_2026}{Hristov et al., 2026};
\citeproc{ref-tomte_genderdifferences_2011}{Tømte \& Hatlevik, 2011}).
Yet, despite these recurrent patterns, such dimensional distinctions
have rarely been subjected to formal tests of construct validity or
cross-national measurement invariance, which limits confidence in
substantive interpretations of gender inequalities or between-country
differences.

When comparing psychological or educational constructs across social
groups or countries, measurement invariance is a critical methodological
requirement. Without evidence that a scale functions similarly across
populations, differences in latent means or associations may reflect
measurement artifacts rather than genuine disparities
(\citeproc{ref-davidov_measurement_2014}{Davidov et al., 2014};
\citeproc{ref-leitgob_measurement_2023}{Leitgöb et al., 2023}). This
concern is particularly relevant for digital self-efficacy, as
cross-national variation in digital experiences, institutional exposure
to ICT, and cultural expectations may shape how students interpret and
respond to self-efficacy items
(\citeproc{ref-gebauer_crosscultural_2021}{Gebauer et al., 2021};
\citeproc{ref-hatlevik_students_2018}{Hatlevik et al., 2018};
\citeproc{ref-senkbeil_ictrelated_2022}{Senkbeil, 2022}). Educational
systems may attach different meanings to similar technology-related
tasks due to differences in curricula, infrastructure, and the social
valuation of technological skills
(\citeproc{ref-annemann_teachers_2025}{Annemann et al., 2025};
\citeproc{ref-conte_unlocking_2025}{Conte et al., 2025};
\citeproc{ref-martin-parraga_selfperception_2023}{Martín-Párraga et al.,
2023}).

While the two-dimensional structure proposed by ICILS has been widely
used and supported in cross-national research
(\citeproc{ref-campos_digital_2024}{Campos \& Scherer, 2024};
\citeproc{ref-gebhardt_gender_2019}{Gebhardt et al., 2019};
\citeproc{ref-karpinski_noncognitive_2023}{Karpiński et al., 2023};
\citeproc{ref-rohatgi_role_2016}{Rohatgi et al., 2016}), and some
studies have applied similar item groupings to PISA data
(\citeproc{ref-hristov_understanding_2026}{Hristov et al., 2026};
\citeproc{ref-tomte_genderdifferences_2011}{Tømte \& Hatlevik, 2011}),
no study has systematically examined whether a comparable bidimensional
digital self-efficacy construct can be established and validated across
both assessments. In addition, although partial cross-national
invariance has been tested for the ICILS digital self-efficacy scales
(\citeproc{ref-campos_digital_2024}{Campos \& Scherer, 2024};
\citeproc{ref-fraillon_iea_2025}{Fraillon et al., 2025}) and gender
invariance has been explored for bidimensional specifications in PISA
(\citeproc{ref-hristov_understanding_2026}{Hristov et al., 2026}),
cross-assessment comparability remains largely unaddressed.

Building on this framework, we test the following hypotheses:

\(H_{1}\): A two-dimensional model of digital self-efficacy (general and
specialized) can be identified in both PISA 2022 and ICILS 2023.

\(H_{2}\): The two-dimensional measurement model of digital
self-efficacy is invariant across countries.

\(H_{3}\): The two-dimensional measurement model of digital
self-efficacy is invariant between girls and boys.

\(H_{4}\): Gender differences in digital self-efficacy are
dimension-specific, such that boys report higher levels of specialized
digital self-efficacy than girls, whereas gender differences in general
digital self-efficacy are smaller or absent.

\section{Methods}\label{methods}

\subsection{Data}\label{data}

ICILS 2023 employed a two-stage cluster sampling approach across 34
countries and more than 130,000 students worldwide. In the first stage,
schools with students in the target grade were randomly selected using a
probability proportional to size (PPS) method. In the second stage, one
intact class was randomly selected within each sampled school, and all
students in that class were invited to participate
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_international_2025}{Fraillon, 2025}).

The PISA 2022 target population consists of 15-year-old students
attending educational establishments and employs a two-stage stratified
sampling design. In the first stage, schools were systematically
selected with PPS from national sampling frames. In the second stage, a
target number of students (typically 42) were randomly selected with
equal probability from within each sampled school
(\citeproc{ref-oecd_pisa_2024}{OECD, 2024}). The ICT questionnarie that
contains the DSE battery was applied in a group of countries. The
official dataset includes approximately 393,000 students, nested within
52 countries.

To ensure comparability, both datasets were filtered to include only
countries that participated in both the PISA ICT questionnaire and the
ICILS survey. Furthermore, specific adjustments were made to maximize
overlap between the samples. In the ICILS data, Germany is reported as
two separate regions reflecting its distinct educational systems; for
this analysis, these regions were aggregated to form a single national
entity. Similarly, ICILS data for Belgium were collected exclusively
from the Flemish Community (Flanders) in this cycle. To align the
datasets, data from Flanders were treated as representative of the
country.

The final country-level sample includes 22 countries, with 91,132
students in ICILS and 183,902 students in PISA.

\subsection{Variables}\label{variables}

Both studies include a module on digital self-efficacy in their
questionnaires. In ICILS the 13 items are introduced with the phrase
\emph{``How well can you do:''} and the recoded categories are 1 = I do
not think I could do this; 2 = I have never done this, but I could work
out how to do it; 3 = Moderately well; 4 = Very well. PISA prefaces its
14-item module with the question \emph{``To what extent are you able to
do the following tasks when using \textless digital
resources\textgreater?''}, where \textless digital
resources\textgreater{} is a placeholder for a term adapted to the
target culture. PISA recoded categories are 1 = I cannot do this; 2 = I
struggle to do this on my own; 3 = I can do this with a bit of effort; 4
= I can easily do this. Responses as ``I don't know'' were treated as
missing values. A complete comparison of the items from both surveys was
presented in Table~\ref{tbl-comparison}, highlighting the concordances
and distinctions between the tasks considered in bot studies, the
Digcomp area to which the task corresponds, and the type of DSE we are
assuming to match the item.

We also consider the three-digit ISO country code and the student's sex
(recoded as 1 = male and 2 = female) from both datasets.

\subsection{Analytical Strategy}\label{analytical-strategy}

The main analyses were performed within a Confirmatory Factor Analysis
(CFA) framework to test the hypothesised two-factor structure of DSE
(\citeproc{ref-brown_confirmatory_2015}{Brown, 2015}). The CFA models
were estimated with the \texttt{lavaan} package
(\citeproc{ref-rosseel_lavaan_2012}{Rosseel, 2012}). Given the ordinal
nature of the Likert-scale items, they were treated as ordered
categorical variables. Therefore, models were estimated using the
Weighted Least Squares Mean and Variance Adjusted (WLSMV) estimator.
Missing data were handled using pairwise deletion, which is the standard
approach for this estimator in lavaan besides the (default) listwise
deletion. In both studies, the complex structure of the samples was
addressed using sample weights (Senate Weights\footnote{In PISA, Senate
  Weights are constructed by the OECD based on the final weight of the
  student, ensuring that each country totals 5000. For ICILS, we follow
  the same procedure based on total weights (TOTWGTS).}). This weighting
was applied to all models and descriptive analyses to ensure
representative parameter estimates.

To evaluate the goodness-of-fit of the models, the chi-square (χ²) test
was used. However, due to the test's sensitivity to large sample sizes,
model evaluation primarily relied on a complementary set of fit indices:
the Comparative Fit Index (CFI), the Tucker-Lewis Index (TLI), and the
Root Mean Square Error of Approximation (RMSEA). Following common
guidelines, CFI and TLI values of .95 or higher are considered
indicative of excellent fit, while values between 0.90 and 0.95 are
considered acceptable. For RMSEA, values of .06 or less indicate a close
fit, while values up to .08 are considered adequate
(\citeproc{ref-brown_confirmatory_2015}{Brown, 2015};
\citeproc{ref-wang_structural_2012}{Wang \& Wang, 2012}). To verify the
reliability of the models, McDonald's \(\omega\) are reported. Values
above .7 indicate good reliability
(\citeproc{ref-hair_multivariate_2009}{Hair, 2009}). In terms of
convergent validity, statistically significant factor loadings of at
least .5 will be required. In addition, an Average Variance Extracted
(AVE) of at least .5 will be required, which means that the model
explains 50\% of the variance of the items on average
(\citeproc{ref-cheung_reporting_2024}{Cheung et al., 2024}). The R
\texttt{semTools} package was used to perform reliability and convergent
validity analyses (\citeproc{ref-jorgensen_semtools_2025}{Jorgensen et
al., 2025}).

The analysis process involved a series of consecutive steps. The first
was to estimate a pooled model to evaluate the fit of the hypothetical
model in each study. In instances where the initial model failed to
achieve an acceptable fit, a principled procedure of model
re-specification was undertaken. Sources of model misfit were diagnosed
by examining multiple indicators, including residual correlation
matrices and modification indices. Items identified as primary sources
of misspecification were considered for removal. However, any
modification was contingent upon a strong theoretical justification
beyond statistical improvement. We critically evaluated items for
potential issues related to ambiguous phrasing, conceptual overlap, or
compromised content validity due to the evolving technological context.
This process was performed judiciously to arrive at a final,
well-fitting model that remained theoretically coherent. A factor was
required to have a minimum of three indicators to be retained.

In the next step, to test the comparability of this measurement model
across countries and genders, a series of Multi-Group Confirmatory
Factor Analyses (MGCFA) was conducted to assess Measurement Equivalence
or Invariance (ME/I) (e.g.,
\citeproc{ref-beaujean_latent_2014}{Beaujean, 2014};
\citeproc{ref-davidov_measurement_2014}{Davidov et al., 2014}). This
procedure involves testing three sequential levels of invariance by
imposing progressive restrictions: \textbf{1) configural invariance},
which tests if the same factor structure holds for all groups;
\textbf{2) metric invariance}, which adds the constraint that factor
loadings are equal across groups; and \textbf{3) scalar invariance},
which further constrains item intercepts (or thresholds for ordinal
items) to be equal (\citeproc{ref-cheung_evaluating_2002}{Cheung \&
Rensvold, 2002}; \citeproc{ref-milfont_testing_2010}{Milfont \& Fischer,
2010}). The establishment of invariance between these nested models was
evaluated by examining the change in CFI (ΔCFI) and RMSEA (ΔRMSEA).
Specifically, for \textbf{metric (weak) invariance}, a change in CFI of
≥ -.004 and a change in RMSEA of ≤ .05 were considered acceptable. For
\textbf{scalar (strong) invariance}, a ΔCFI of ≥ -.004 and a ΔRMSEA of ≤
.01 were considered adequate
(\citeproc{ref-rutkowski_measurement_2017}{Rutkowski \& Svetina, 2017}).
Achieving metric invariance is a prerequisite for further analysis of
the association among dimensions of DSE with factors. Achieving scalar
invariance is a prerequisite for further analysis of the association
among dimensions of DSE with factors and estimation of the latent means
across countries and across gender.

Upon establishing scalar invariance, mean scores of General and
Specialised DSE country distribution are explored. In the next step, the
magnitude of the DSE gender gap is visualised for each country.

All data management and analyses were conducted using the R statistical
environment. The hypotheses were previously pre-registered on
\href{https://osf.io/fxhc5/overview}{Open Science Framework}, and the
code analyses are available on
\href{https://github.com/milenio-nudos/picils_dse}{Github}.

\section{Results}\label{results}

The results are organized into three sequential sections. The first
section reports the assessment of the global measurement model and the
subsequent invariance testing for the ICILS dataset. The second section
presents the equivalent analysis for the PISA dataset, including model
specification, fit, and invariance results. Finally, the third section
provides the descriptive findings for both studies, focusing on the
distribution of scores and gender disparities across the participating
education systems.

\subsection{Study 1: ICILS}\label{study-1-icils}

\subsubsection{Global Measurement Model
Assessment}\label{global-measurement-model-assessment}

The initial model for ICILS exhibited a suboptimal fit to the data, with
an RMSEA exceeding the acceptable threshold of .08 (\(RMSEA = .088\)).
Consequently, the model was respecified based on theory-consistent
diagnostics (modification indices and residual correlations). Two items
were removed due to evidence of cross-loadings and local dependence to
improve parsimony and interpretability. The first problematic item was
`Find the original sources of information referred to in an article on
the internet,' which displayed a substantial modification index
(\(MI = 13,668.28\)) driven by a cross-loading on the Specialized DSE
factor. Additionally, this item showed strong positive residual
correlations with technical indicators, suggesting that students may
interpret source verification as a more demanding digital literacy task
that overlaps with specialized competencies. We proceeded to remove this
item from the General DSE factor; the empirical overlap suggests that
the item may tap a broader content-creation component. Subsequently,
although the item `Build or edit a webpage' showed a high modification
index (\(MI = 4,707.52\)) due to a cross-loading on the General factor,
it was retained to preserve model identification and maintain at least
three indicators per factor. Instead, we removed the item `Create a
multi-media presentation (with sound, pictures, or video),' which
exhibited a notable modification index (\(MI = 3,982.96\)) and strong
positive residual correlations with `Develop webpage.' The high
covariance indicates that students perceive multimedia creation and web
development as a unified dimension of `content creation', a finding that
aligns with the DigComp framework (see Table~\ref{tbl-comparison} `3.1
Develop Digital Content'). A detailed table presenting all modification
indices and residual correlations for the ICILS respecification process
is provided in the Appendix (Table~\ref{tbl-mod-indices-icils} and
Table~\ref{tbl-residuals-icils}).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/icils_model.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-1}ICILS global model}

\end{figure}%

Following the respecification, the final ICILS measurement model yielded
fit indices indicative of a good fit to the data
(\(\chi^2 = 20,523.816\); \(df=43\); \(p < .001\); \(CFI = .979\);
\(TLI = .973\); \(RMSEA = .074\)). Standardized factor loadings were
substantial and statistically significant for all indicators, ranging
from .63 to .82. This implies that the latent factors account for
between 39.5\% and 67.6\% of the variance in the observed items
(\(R^2\)). Regarding psychometric quality, both dimensions exhibited
adequate internal consistency and convergent validity, with statistics
surpassing the recommended cutoffs (General DSE: \(\omega = .88\),
\(AVE = .55\); Specialized DSE: \(\omega = .80\), \(AVE = .62\)).
Finally, the estimation revealed a moderate correlation of .51 between
the two factors, reinforcing the discriminant validity of the construct.

Testing}\label{measurement-invariance-testing}

\global\setlength{\arrayrulewidth}{\Oldarrayrulewidth}

\global\setlength{\tabcolsep}{\Oldtabcolsep}

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

As presented in Table~\ref{tbl-invariance-icils}, the ICILS measurement
model achieved configural and metric invariance across participating
countries. However, scalar invariance could not be established, as the
decrease in \(CFI\) (\(\Delta CFI = -.007\)) exceeded the strict cutoff
criterion. While establishing metric invariance confirms that the factor
loadings are comparable across educational systems, the lack of scalar
invariance indicates non-equivalence in item intercepts. In contrast,
the assessment of gender invariance yielded positive results; the model
satisfied all three levels of invariance (configural, metric, and
scalar), with changes in fit indices remaining well within acceptable
ranges. Detailed information regarding factor loadings for the General
and Specialized DSE dimensions, along with the \(\chi^2\) contribution
by country derived from the scalar invariance model, is provided in the
Appendix (Table~\ref{tbl-loadings-chi-icils}) to examine potential
sources of misfit across countries.

\subsection{Study 2: PISA}\label{study-2-pisa}

\subsubsection{Model Respecification and Global
Fit}\label{model-respecification-and-global-fit}

The initial PISA model exhibited substantial misfit (\(RMSEA = .12\)),
primarily driven by two items that displayed strong cross-loadings and
multiple significant residual covariances. The first item, ``Select the
most efficient programme or App that allows me to carry out a specific
task'', showed a substantial modification index for the General factor
(\(MI = 76,502.42\)) and problematic negative residual correlations with
items from the Specialized dimension (e.g., ``logical solution'',
``programming''). Additionally, it showed a positive residual
correlation with ``privacy settings'', an indicator of the General DSE
factor. Consequently, this item was removed; contrary to the theoretical
model, the data suggest this task is perceived by students as a general
competency rather than one related to the technical problem-solving
nature of the specialized dimension.

The second problematic item was `Create, update and maintain a webpage
or a blog,' which also presented a strong cross-loading on the General
factor (\(MI = 39,013.99\)). Crucially, it exhibited its strongest
positive residual correlation with `create media.' This specific
covariance mirrors the pattern observed in the ICILS respecification,
indicating that students perceive web page development and multimedia
creation as a unified dimension of `content creation,' a finding aligned
with the DigComp framework. Furthermore, unlike ICILS, the PISA item
phrasing explicitly references a `blog,' which further anchors the task
in non-technical content creation contexts. Based on these consistent
empirical and theoretical grounds across both studies, this item was
removed from the PISA analysis. In the appendix, a comprehensive table
details the modification indices and residual correlations that informed
the PISA model respecification process (Table~\ref{tbl-mod-indices-pisa}
and Table~\ref{tbl-residuals-pisa}).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/pisa_model.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-2}PISA respecified global model}

\end{figure}%

Following this respecification, the PISA model demonstrated a
satisfactory fit to the data (\(\chi^2 = 37,625.623\); \(df=53\);
\(p < .001\); \(CFI = .994\); \(TLI = .992\); \(RMSEA = .068\)). Factor
loadings were high and statistically significant, ranging from .78 to
.93, thus explaining between 60\% and 86\% of the item variance
(\(R^2\)). Additionally, both factors demonstrated strong reliability
and convergent validity, exceeding all established thresholds (General
DSE: \(\omega = .95\), \(AVE = .71\); Specialized DSE: \(\omega = .89\),
\(AVE = .80\)). The inter-factor correlation was moderate at .49,
further supporting the distinctness of the dimensions.

\subsubsection{Measurement Invariance
Testing}\label{measurement-invariance-testing-1}

As detailed in Table~\ref{tbl-invariance-pisa}, the PISA measurement
model successfully established invariance across all three tested levels
(configural, metric, and scalar) for both country and gender groupings.
The changes in the key fit indices (\(\Delta CFI\) and \(\Delta RMSEA\))
between the nested models remained strictly within the recommended
thresholds. These results confirm the cross-cultural and cross-gender
comparability of the DSE construct within the PISA dataset, thereby
satisfying the necessary psychometric prerequisites for comparative
analyses. Consistent with the reporting for ICILS, a detailed table
presenting the factor loadings and country-specific \(\chi^2\)
contributions derived from the scalar invariance model is provided in
the Appendix (Table~\ref{tbl-loadings-chi-pisa}).

\subsection{Country and Gender Differences in
DSE}\label{country-and-gender-differences-in-dse}

This section presents the descriptive analyses by country and gender for
both the PISA and ICILS datasets. To ensure that the results accurately
reflect the underlying population structures, all descriptive statistics
were calculated applying the final sampling weights for each study
(Senate Weights). A methodological distinction regarding comparability
is necessary: while PISA established scalar invariance (allowing for
strict mean comparisons), ICILS only achieved metric invariance.
Consequently, the absolute ranking of countries in ICILS should be
interpreted with caution and regarded as exploratory. However, we opted
to utilize averaged indices for both studies to maintain analytical
consistency. A robustness check conducted for both datasets confirmed
that the averaged indices and latent mean scores correlated above .95
across all participating countries (see
Table~\ref{tbl-index-latent-means-icils} and
Table~\ref{tbl-index-latent-means-pisa} in the Appendix). Furthermore,
employing averaged indices preserves the original response scale,
facilitating the substantive interpretation and enabling a direct
comparison between the General and Specialized dimensions across both
datasets.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/icils_country_dse_comparison.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-3}ICILS country DSE scores}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/pisa_country_dse_comparison.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-4}PISA country DSE scores}

\end{figure}%

As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig-3} (ICILS) and Figure~\ref{fig-4}
(PISA), a highly consistent pattern emerges across all participating
education systems in both studies: students systematically report higher
levels of General DSE compared to Specialized DSE. Regarding the
country-level distribution for the Specialized dimension, the results
from both assessments reveal a striking counter-intuitive finding.
Education systems often characterized by developing digital
infrastructure relative to the OECD average report the highest aggregate
specialized self-efficacy; this is evident in the prominent position of
Kazakhstan and Romania in ICILS, and for Kazakhstan in PISA. In sharp
contrast, countries with advanced digital ecosystems and traditionally
strong performance on Computer and Information Literacy (CIL)
assessments ---such as Germany, Austria, and Denmark--- rank at the very
bottom of the distribution in both studies. This inversion may reflect
differences in self evaluative standards, task interpretation, or
reference group effects across countries.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/icils_gender_dse_gaps.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-5}ICILS gender gaps by country}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/pisa_gender_dse_gaps.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-6}PISA gender gaps by country}

\end{figure}%

Consistent with prior literature
(\citeproc{ref-gebhardt_gender_2019}{Gebhardt et al., 2019}), the
analysis reveal distinct gender patterns across the two dimensions of
digital self-efficacy, replicated in both assessments. As illustrated in
Figure~\ref{fig-5} and Figure~\ref{fig-6}, female students generally
report higher levels of self-efficacy in the General dimension; however,
these differences are relatively modest in magnitude. The Specialized
dimension reveals a pronounced and consistent gap favoring male students
across almost all participating education systems. Exceptions to this
trend are observed in countries like Kazakhstan or Chile, where the
gender gap in the specialized dimension is minimal. An examination of
the country-specific magnitudes reveals a counter-intuitive pattern
regarding the relationship between national development contexts and
these disparities. Education systems traditionally associated with
higher levels of gender equality and digital development, such as
Germany, Denmark, and Finland, exhibit considerably larger gender gaps
in specialized self-efficacy compared to the aforementioned developing
contexts.

\section{Discussion}\label{discussion}

The present study examined whether a bidimensional conceptualization of
digital self-efficacy (DSE), distinguishing between General and
Specialized DSE, can be identified and meaningfully compared within two
major international large-scale assessments, ICILS 2023 and PISA 2022.
Overall, the findings support the feasibility of recovering a comparable
two-factor structure in both datasets, indicating that students'
perceived capability to perform routine digital tasks is empirically
distinct from their perceived capability in more specialized activities
(supporting \(H_1\)). Although achieving this comparability required
targeted model refinements in both studies, the observed patterns of
misfit offer substantive insights into how adolescents interpret and
classify digital tasks in contemporary digital environments.

A first contribution of the study concerns measurement validity and
model specification. In both ICILS and PISA, a small set of items
exhibited evidence of cross-loadings and local dependence, suggesting
that certain digital tasks do not map neatly onto a purely
complexity-based distinction between general versus specialized
activities. In particular, tasks involving information, communication,
and content creation showed consistent empirical overlap, pointing to a
component that blurs the boundaries between the two dimensions. For
instance, while in previous assessment cycles web page development was
considered a distinctive skill of specialized technology users
(\citeproc{ref-fraillon_icils_2015}{Fraillon et al., 2015}), the present
results indicate that it now shares substantial variance with the latent
construct of General DSE. These findings do not contradict the
bidimensional structure itself; rather, they suggest that the
interpretation of what constitutes ``specialized'' digital engagement
may be shifting over time. This shift has important implications for the
temporal stability of DSE operationalizations in large-scale
assessments.

The observed patterns are consistent with recent developments in digital
studies. The diffusion of user-friendly platforms for website building
and online publishing has reduced the technical barriers associated with
activities that historically required specialized knowledge
(\citeproc{ref-jagtap_no_2024}{Jagtap, 2024};
\citeproc{ref-lively_integrating_2023}{Lively et al., 2023}). At the
same time, rising standards for sophisticated multimedia production,
potentially intensified by social media dynamics and AI-supported
content generation, may alter students' perceptions of difficulty for
tasks previously classified as general digital competence
(\citeproc{ref-alnaser_impact_2024}{Alnaser, 2024};
\citeproc{ref-gardan_usergenerated_2022}{Gârdan et al., 2022}).
Together, these contextual shifts provide a plausible explanation for
why certain tasks migrate across the boundary between General and
Specialized DSE, underscoring the need for DSE measurement frameworks to
remain responsive to technological change.

The findings also contribute to an ongoing conceptual debate regarding
the dimensionality of DSE. Recent proposals advocate for
multidimensional DSE measures aligned with competence frameworks such as
DigComp
(\citeproc{ref-europeancommissionjointresearchcentre_digcomp_2022}{European
Commission Joint Research Centre, 2022};
\citeproc{ref-ulfert-blank_assessing_2022}{Ulfert-Blank \& Schmidt,
2022}), which organizes digital competence into domains including
information and data literacy, communication and collaboration, content
creation, safety, and problem solving. While this approach offers a
theoretically rich alternative to complexity-based distinctions, the
item batteries available in ICILS and PISA are currently too limited to
support a psychometrically stable five-dimensional model, and
large-scale validation remains scarce. Within these constraints, the
present study provides a parsimonious and empirically supported
bidimensional solution that is feasible with existing ILSA data and
enables systematic comparisons across assessments.

Turning to comparability across groups, invariance results differed
across assessments. In PISA, the bidimensional model achieved
configural, metric, and scalar invariance across countries, providing
strong evidence that the construct is comparable across participating
education systems (supporting \(H_2\)). In ICILS, the model reached
configural and metric invariance but failed to meet scalar invariance
criteria across countries, indicating that cross-country mean
differences should be interpreted with caution and underscoring the
importance of explicitly testing between-country comparability before
drawing substantive conclusions (partial support for \(H_2\)). These
results reinforce previous evidence of bi-dimensionality on PISA
(\citeproc{ref-hristov_understanding_2026}{Hristov et al., 2026}), and
suggest that even when a common two-dimensional structure is
recoverable, the degree of cross-national comparability may depend on
assessment-specific design features and item functioning.

In contrast, gender invariance results were consistently supportive
across both assessments. Scalar invariance was established for gender in
both ICILS and PISA, indicating that General and Specialized DSE can be
meaningfully compared between girls and boys within each dataset
(supporting \(H_3\)). This finding strengthens the validity of gender
comparisons in DSE research and suggests that observed gender gaps are
unlikely to be artefacts of measurement non-equivalence, at least within
the tested frameworks.

Beyond measurement considerations, the descriptive patterns provide
substantive insights into digital inequality. Across countries and in
both studies, students reported higher levels of General DSE than
Specialized DSE, consistent with the notion that routine digital
activities are more widely internalized than specialized or
computational competencies. Gender differences, consistenly with
previous findings (\citeproc{ref-gebhardt_gender_2019}{Gebhardt et al.,
2019}), were clearly dimension-specific: girls tended to report slightly
higher General DSE, whereas boys consistently reported substantially
higher Specialized DSE across most education systems. This pattern
indicates that gender disparities in digital self-efficacy depend
critically on the type of digital task domain being assessed (supporting
\(H_4\)).

At the country level, the magnitude of gender gaps in Specialized DSE
varied considerably across education systems, and in some contexts these
gaps were minimal. This variability suggests that gender differentiation
in specialized DSE is context-dependent rather than universal,
supporting \(H_4\) from a cross-national perspective. In addition,
exploratory country-level distributions revealed that higher levels of
Specialized DSE and smaller specialized gender gaps were not necessarily
concentrated in highly digitalized contexts. This counterintuitive
pattern aligns with prior evidence indicating that digital self-efficacy
does not map straightforwardly onto objective performance indicators at
the country level (\citeproc{ref-rozman_students_2025}{Rožman et al.,
2025}), as well as with findings related to the gender equality paradox,
which show that gender gaps in STEM-related attitudes can be larger in
more gender-equal societies (\citeproc{ref-guo_crosscultural_2024}{Guo
et al., 2024}; \citeproc{ref-marsh_illusory_2021}{Marsh et al., 2021}).

\section{Conclusions}\label{conclusions}

In sum, the study provides evidence that a two-dimensional
representation of DSE is recoverable in both ICILS and PISA (\(H_1\)),
that cross-national comparability is stronger in PISA than in ICILS
(partial support for \(H_2\)), that gender comparisons are supported in
both assessments (\(H_3\)), and that gender and country differences
operate in dimension-specific ways (\(H_4\)). Together, these findings
highlight the importance of explicitly testing measurement assumptions
when using ILSA data to interpret digital inequalities and to accumulate
evidence across international assessments. Future research should
examine whether these results reflect reference group effects,
cross-cultural differences in calibration, or variation in exposure to
and social valuation of specialized digital pathways.

The article provides future researchers with the opportunity to
investigate DSE in both ILSAs using a common measurement strategy, while
also pointing to three main limitations of the proposed approach. First,
achieving acceptable model fit required the removal of a small number of
items from the DSE batteries in both assessments, which may reduce
content coverage and limit direct comparability with official reporting.
Second, cross-national invariance in ICILS did not reach the most
restrictive level, indicating that country-level mean comparisons should
be interpreted with caution. Third, the analyses relied on a single
cycle from each assessment (PISA 2022 and ICILS 2023), which prevents an
evaluation of the temporal stability of the measurement models. Future
research should extend this approach across multiple cycles to assess
whether the two-dimensional structure remains robust as digital
environments evolve. In addition, assessment developers may consider
expanding and updating DSE item pools to better capture emerging domains
of digital competence in rapidly changing educational contexts.
